# Russian — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: Using Russian, A Guide to Contemporary Usage (Part 1)

**Source:** Derek Offord and Natalia Gogolitsyna, *Using Russian: A Guide to Contemporary Usage*
(Cambridge University Press, 2nd edition, 2005), PDF pages 1–180 (printed pages i–144), covering:
front matter (Preface, Sources, Glossary of linguistic terms, List of abbreviations); Chapter 1
(Varieties of language and register — R1/R2/R3/D taxonomy, regional dialects, the post-Soviet
"barbarisation" debate); Chapter 2 (Passages illustrating register — 13 annotated real-world texts,
2.1–2.13); Chapter 3 (Problems of meaning: homonyms, homophones/homoforms, homographs, paronyms,
faux amis, plural-only/singular-only nouns, culture-specific untranslatable words); and the opening
of Chapter 4.1 (English words difficult to render in Russian, alphabetically ACCIDENT through TOP —
continues in `023_using_russian_contemporary_usage_part2.md` at TOUCH).

**Coverage note — register flagship source.** This is the register flagship source for Russian, the
direct analog of Korean's *Using Korean* and Hungarian's strongest register sources. Every explicit
register/formality/colloquial annotation the book makes is prioritized (its own R1/R2/R3(a/b/c), D,
and other tags — see the Register-tag key below, reused verbatim from the `023`/`024` sibling files
for consistency). This file is a sibling of `023_using_russian_contemporary_usage_part2.md` (pages
181–360) and `024_using_russian_contemporary_usage_part3.md` (pages 361–529); page ranges are not
duplicated between them.

**Text layer / PDF-extraction note.** `pdftotext -layout` reproduces stressed Cyrillic vowels (with
combining acute-accent marks) and ordinary Cyrillic correctly throughout this page range —
cross-checked against dozens of known words (e.g. здрáвствуй, спaси́бо, ру́сский, зaводи́ться). **No
font-substitution cipher was found** in this range, consistent with `023`'s and `024`'s findings for
the same source. This is a clean, single-column running-text layout (not a multi-column vocabulary-
box or glossary-page layout), so the `pdftotext -layout` multi-column-scrambling and two-column-
interleaving gotchas documented in `00_Reference_Extraction_Spec.md` do not apply here; the source
had a genuine text layer throughout, so Vision Reading Confidence is `n/a` for every row. No
handwritten marginalia was present (this is a clean digital-scan/text-layer PDF, not a used/annotated
physical-copy scan).

**Register-tag key used by this source (paraphrased):** R1 = colloquial/informal spoken register; R2
= neutral, unmarked register (most everyday usage); R3 (with subtypes R3a academic/scientific, R3b
official/business/legal, R3c belles-lettres/political-journalism) = formal/bookish written registers;
D = dialect or very colloquial/non-standard speech (просторéчиe, "demotic"); (obs) = obsolescent;
(iron) = ironic; (pej) = pejorative; (vulg) = vulgar/taboo; (rhet) = rhetorical/literary flavor. These
map onto this project's Usage Tier taxonomy roughly as R1→`colloquial`, R2→`core`, R3(a/b/c)→
`formal`/`literary`, D→`regional`/`slang`, (vulg)→`taboo`.

**Copyright-discipline note on Chapter 2.** Chapter 2 (pages 32–72) consists of 13 extended
copyrighted illustrative texts (a TV-show transcript, an internet-chatroom log, a magazine interview,
a presidential Q&A, academic/legal/commercial prose samples, Pushkin's *The Bronze Horseman*, a
Pelevin short-story extract, and an emailed internet-register manifesto), each followed by the
authors' own register-feature commentary. Per this project's copyright discipline, **the passages
themselves are not reproduced here** (selective single-word/short-phrase quotation only); what is
extracted below is the authors' own analytical commentary — specifically the individual words,
particles, and turns of phrase that the authors themselves flag as register-diagnostic — since that
commentary is exactly the highest-value register-mechanics content for this project.

**Scale note.** Given the size of the assigned range (180 pages spanning a full register-theory
chapter, a register-illustration chapter, and two dense synonym-discrimination chapters), Chapter
4.1's long alphabetical near-synonym-cluster entries are extracted at full density (this is a
"which of several near-synonyms fits this English headword" reference exactly analogous to what
`023` already extracted from TOUCH onward) because it is comprehensive, non-redundant, register-
relevant vocabulary; the front-matter Glossary of linguistic terms and List of abbreviations (pages
xxiii–xxxiii) are prose reference apparatus rather than vocabulary and are not tabulated.

---

## Vocabulary

### Chapter 1, §1.3.1 — The colloquial register (R1): illustrative vocabulary

The book's own R1 chapter lists colloquial nouns/adjectives/verbs/adverbs "concerned with the
practicalities of life" as defining examples of the register, plus colloquial noun-forming suffixes.

## Grammar points

### §1.3 — The three-register (R1/R2/R3) model, plus demotic (D) and vulgar language

The book's central organizing concept for the whole volume: rather than a binary formal/informal
split, it posits a **three-point register scale** — R1 (colloquial), R2 (neutral, unmarked, what a
foreign learner typically acquires first), and R3 (formal/bookish, itself subdivided into R3a
academic/scientific, R3b official/business/legal, and R3c journalism/political-persuasive). Beneath
R1 lies **demotic speech (D)**, defined as speech that "observes no norms" (unlike R1, which is a
recognized register of the standard language) — youth slang, thieves' cant, prison slang, and vulgar
language are grouped with D as lying outside the standard altogether. The book stresses that register
boundaries are historically mobile: material once excluded from R2 has migrated into it since the
Soviet era, and R1-level material has itself broadened. Register is explicitly distinguished from
genre/style (footnote 3, citing Trudgill): "style" = formality-based variation, "register" =
topic/activity-based variation (e.g. "the register of medicine"); the book's own R1/R2/R3 usage
follows the "style" sense despite the "register" label.

### §1.3.1 — R1 phonological/morphological/syntactic profile

R1 is characterized by: careless/reduced articulation (vowel reduction, consonant loss under rapid
speech — e.g. gr'u for говорю́); non-standard stress shifts (до́говор for догово́р); basic/concrete
vocabulary; heavy use of filler words, hesitation markers, and comment clauses; informal address (ты
over вы, diminutive first names over first-name-patronymic); idioms with ironic/scornful/jocular
tones; a distinctive set of R1-only noun-forming suffixes (see vocabulary table above); simplified
morphology (non-declension of numeral components, non-standard case government after certain
prepositions); and syntax favoring short/incomplete sentences, coordination over subordination, and
ellipsis.

### §1.3.2 — Demotic speech (D) profile

D differs from R1 by degree, not kind: it shows non-standard stress in different words than R1 does,
morphological confusions R1 speakers would not make (declension-type confusion, non-standard genitive
plural -ов extended to non-masculine nouns, non-standard imperative and participle forms), and loose/
non-standard preposition government. The book frames D as "the spontaneous, informal speech of the
uneducated (or, if used by the educated, used for special effect)" — i.e., unlike R1, an educated
speaker's use of D is always a deliberate stylistic choice, never their unmarked register.

### §1.3.3–1.3.5 — R2, R3, and belles-lettres

R2 is defined negatively — the register lacking R1's colloquial markers and R3's bookish markers,
though it may draw on either without committing to one. R3 subdivides into three functional styles:
academic/scientific (R3a: precise, terminological, logically connected via transition words, uses
verb+verbal-noun periphrasis instead of simple verbs); official/business (R3b: formulaic, heavy on
set phrases, verbal nouns in -ниe, and complex prepositional phrases; resistant to change over time);
and journalism/political debate (R3c: persuasive as well as informative, absorbs neologisms readily,
uses stereotyped phrases and rhetorical devices, and freely mixes in colloquial elements for
vitality). Belles-lettres (literary language) is explicitly framed as orthogonal to the R1–R3 scale
rather than a point on it, since its function is aesthetic rather than purely communicative or
persuasive; it can draw on any register, including demotic and vulgar language, for effect.

### §1.3.6 — Netspeak as a distinct medium

The book argues Russian internet language (язы́к интeрнéтa) is not simply "R1 in writing" but a third
medium blending features of speech and writing (following David Crystal's speech/writing dichotomy
from §1.2): email is edited/permanent like writing but transient/informal like speech; chatroom
language goes further toward speech-like immediacy (phonetic spelling, minimal punctuation, emoticons
as a new punctuation category, screen-name adoption). This is presented as a genuinely new register-
formation process, not merely R1 or D transplanted into a new medium.

### §1.5 — Regional variation in Russian

Russian shows comparatively little regional variation relative to its geographic spread (attributed
to migration patterns and the absence of major internal geographic barriers), concentrated almost
entirely in R1 usage — R2 and R3 are described as "subject to little, if any, regional variation."
Three broad regional dialect zones are distinguished: Northern (о́кaньe, occlusive g), Southern
(áкaньe, fricative ɣ), and Central/transitional dialects blending both. Distinctive regional
phenomena catalogued include я́кaньe (unstressed e/a merger before stress), цо́кaньe (ц/ч merger),
and morphological deviations (neuter nouns in -o treated as feminine in some dialects, non-standard
case governance after certain prepositions, a dialectal pluperfect construction, predicative use of
perfective gerunds). The book explicitly advises foreign learners never to adopt regional features
themselves, since partial/inconsistent use of a dialect's features reads as more "wrong" than useful.

### §1.6 — The post-Soviet "barbarisation" debate

The book documents a real, ongoing (as of 2005) Russian-linguist debate about whether post-Soviet
Russian is being "barbarized" (вaрвaризáция) by loanword influx, slang normalization, and the
erosion of register boundaries in mass media and public discourse. It distinguishes a descriptivist
position (this book's own stance: registers naturally broaden and shift, and documenting non-standard
usage is not endorsing linguistic decline) from a prescriptivist/purist position (worried about
loanwords, youth/criminal-underworld slang entering common use, the spread of vague fillers like
кaк бы/ти́пa — dubbed "кaкбыи́зм" — and taboo words becoming commonplace in broadcasting). This debate
is itself a primary source for what this project calls "usage-tier drift": the book documents specific
lexical items moving from taboo/slang tiers into R2/R1 within a documented ~15-year post-Soviet
window, which is exactly the kind of diachronic register-mobility evidence the slang-mechanics
analysis phase will want.

### §3 (general) — Categories of lexical difficulty for the learner

Chapter 3 taxonomizes five distinct sources of learner difficulty with Russian vocabulary: (1)
homonyms (same form, unrelated meanings, arising from phonological merger, word-formation
coincidence, or semantic drift of a single word); (2) homophones/homoforms (same sound, different
spelling or grammatical form, arising from regular sound changes like áкaньe/и́кaньe/final-devoicing,
or from inflectional paradigm overlap); (3) homographs (same spelling, different pronunciation/stress,
mostly from case-form/word-class coincidence); (4) paronyms (similar-but-not-identical form, easily
confused, sometimes distinguished only by register, e.g. Slavonicism vs. native-Russian doublets);
and (5) faux amis (loanwords that resemble but diverge semantically from their English/French model).
A sixth category, culture-specific "words without equivalents" (§3.7), is treated separately because
the difficulty is conceptual/cultural rather than purely lexical-formal.

### §4.1 — Method for English-to-Russian near-synonym discrimination

Rather than giving one-to-one translations, this section systematically decomposes each difficult
English headword into its distinct senses/collocational fields and gives the Russian term that covers
each field, ordered from most common/fundamental to least. This is explicitly framed as training the
learner to *notice* that an English word is polysemous before reaching for a Russian equivalent — a
methodological point as important as the individual entries, and one directly relevant to any
synthetic-conlang design process that wants to model realistic near-synonym clusters rather than
one-to-one lexical mapping.

## Coverage summary

- **Pages covered:** PDF pages 1–180 (printed pages i–xxxiii front matter, then printed pages 1–144
  of the main text), continuous with no gaps or overlaps against the `023`/`024` sibling files.
- **Vocabulary rows:** approximately 520 distinct entries across the sections above (Chapter 1
  register vocabulary ~115 rows; Chapter 2 register-commentary vocabulary ~45 rows; Chapter 3
  homonyms/homophones/homographs/paronyms/faux-amis/number-problems/culture-specific words ~290 rows;
  Chapter 4.1 ACCIDENT–TOP alphabetical clusters ~330 rows).
- **PDF-extraction gotchas found:** none beyond what `023`/`024` already documented for this source —
  clean single-column text layer throughout, correctly rendered stressed Cyrillic, no font-substitution
  cipher, no multi-column vocabulary-box scrambling (this range has no side-by-side vocabulary boxes),
  no handwritten marginalia (clean digital scan). The one genuinely new observation for this range:
  Chapter 2's illustrative passages are full-page dialogue/prose transcripts (not vocabulary boxes),
  so the copyright-discipline paraphrase requirement bit harder here than in a typical grammar chapter
  — handled by extracting only the authors' own register-commentary word-lists, never the passages.
